Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\nMotion energy, normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. gaussian_filter1d\n\n# ============ PARAMETERS ============\nDATA_DIR = 'data'\nOUTPUT_FILE = 'converted_data.pkl'\nSAMPLE_OUTPUT_FILE = 'sample_data.pkl'\n\nMICE = ['jm031', 'jm032', 'jm038', 'jm039', 'jm040', 'jm046']\n\nBIN_SIZE = 10           # temporal binning factor (10 frames averaged)\nFS = 30                 # imaging frame rate (Hz)\nNEUCOEFF = 0.7          # neuropil correction coefficient (Suite2p default)\nWIN_BASELINE_SEC = 60   # baseline window in seconds\nPRCTILE_BASELINE = 8    # baseline percentile (Suite2p default)\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 120  # 2-minute trial blocks (as in paper's decoding)\nN_OUTPUT_BINS = 5       # number of motion energy bins (quintiles)\n\n# Derived\nBIN_DURATION_MS = (BIN_SIZE / FS) * 1000  # ~333.33 ms\nTRIAL_BINS = int(TRIAL_DURATION_SEC * FS / BIN_SIZE)  # 360 bins per trial\n\n\ndef get_sessions(mouse_dir):\n    \"\"\"Get sorted session directories for a mouse.\"\"\"\n    sessions = [d for d in os.listdir(mouse_dir)\n                if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(mouse_dir, d)) and d[0] == '2']\n    sessions.sort()\n    return sessions\n\n\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu):\n    \"\"\"\n    Compute dF/F using Suite2p default parameters.\n    1. Neuropil correction: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu\n    2. Baseline: Suite2p default 'maximin' method\n       - Gaussian smooth, then min filter, then max filter over 60s window\n    3. dF/F = (Fc - F0) / F0\n    \"\"\"\n    Fc = F - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\n    win = int(WIN_BASELINE_SEC * FS)\n    # Suite2p default 'maximin' baseline\n    Flow = gaussian_filter1d(Fc.astype(np.float64), sigma=win / 6, axis=1)\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\n    F0 = maximum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\n    # Avoid division by zero/near-zero\n    F0_safe = np.where(np.abs(F0) < 1e-6, 1e-6, F0)\n    dff = (Fc - F0) / F0_safe\n    return dff\n\n\ndef align_motion_energy(me, interframe_int, n_neural_frames):\n    \"\"\"\n    Align motion energy to neural frames by detecting and interpolating dropped video frames.\n    Uses interframe_int to detect gaps (> 1.5x median interval = dropped frame).\n    \"\"\"\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n\n    median_ifi = np.median(interframe_int)\n\n    # Build aligned array by inserting NaN at dropped frame locations\n    aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\n    neural_idx = 0\n    for i in range(len(me)):\n        if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\n            aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\n        neural_idx += 1\n        # Check if next interval indicates dropped frames\n        if i < len(interframe_int):\n            n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\n            neural_idx += n_dropped\n\n    # Interpolate NaN values\n    nans = np.isnan(aligned)\n    if np.any(nans) and not np.all(nans):\n        x = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\n        aligned[nans] = np.interp(x[nans], x[~nans], aligned[~nans])\n\n    return aligned\n\n\ndef bin_array(data, bin_size):\n    \"\"\"Average data over consecutive bins along the last axis.\"\"\"\n    if data.ndim == 1:\n        n_bins = len(data) // bin_size\n        return data[:n_bins * bin_size].reshape(n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=1)\n    else:\n        n_bins = data.shape[1] // bin_size\n        return data[:, :n_bins * bin_size].reshape(data.shape[0], n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=2)\n\n\ndef split_into_trials(data, trial_length):\n    \"\"\"Split data into non-overlapping trials of fixed length along last axis.\"\"\"\n    if data.ndim == 1:\n        n_trials = len(data) // trial_length\n        return [data[i*trial_length:(i+1)*trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n    else:\n        n_trials = data.shape[1] // trial_length\n        return [data[:, i*trial_length:(i+1)*trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\n\n\ndef convert_data(data_dir=DATA_DIR, output_file=OUTPUT_FILE, sample_output_file=SAMPLE_OUTPUT_FILE,\n                 sample_only=False):\n    \"\"\"Main conversion function.\"\"\"\n\n    print(\"=\" * 60)\n    print(\"Converting Track2p data to decoder format\")\n    print(\"=\" * 60)\n\n    neural_all = []\n    input_all = []\n    output_all_raw = []  # store raw ME values before discretization\n    subjects = MICE[:]\n    subject_idx = []\n    brain_region_idx_all = []\n\n    # First pass: load, process, bin all data\n    all_me_values = []  # collect all binned ME for global percentile computation\n\n    for mouse_i, mouse in enumerate(MICE):\n        mouse_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, mouse)\n        sessions = get_sessions(mouse_dir)\n        print(f\"\\n--- Mouse {mouse} ({len(sessions)} sessions) ---\")\n\n        for sess_name in sessions:\n            sess_dir = os.path.join(mouse_dir, sess_name)\n            suite2p_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n            move_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'move_deve')\n\n            # Load neural data\n            F = np.load(os.path.join(suite2p_dir, 'F.npy'))\n            Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(suite2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))\n            n_neurons, n_frames = F.shape\n\n            # Compute dF/F\n            dff = compute_dff(F, Fneu)\n\n            # Load behavioral data\n            me_raw = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n            ifi = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'interframe_int.npy'))\n\n            # Align ME to neural frames (handle dropped video frames)\n            me_aligned = align_motion_energy(me_raw, ifi, n_frames)\n\n            # Bin both neural and ME by BIN_SIZE frames\n            dff_binned = bin_array(dff, BIN_SIZE)\n            me_binned = bin_array(me_aligned, BIN_SIZE)\n\n            n_bins = dff_binned.shape[1]\n            n_trials = n_bins // TRIAL_BINS\n\n            print(f\"  {sess_name}: {n_neurons} neurons, {n_frames} frames -> \"\n                  f\"{n_bins} bins -> {n_trials} trials of {TRIAL_BINS} bins\")\n\n            # Split into trials\n            neural_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, TRIAL_BINS)\n            me_trials = split_into_trials(me_binned, TRIAL_BINS)\n\n            # Create time input for each trial (time elapsed from start of session, in seconds)\n            input_trials = []\n            for t_i in range(n_trials):\n                start_sec = t_i * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n                time_bins = np.linspace(\n                    start_sec + BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,  # center of first bin\n                    start_sec + TRIAL_DURATION_SEC - BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,  # center of last bin\n                    TRIAL_BINS\n                )\n                input_trials.append(time_bins.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.float32))\n\n            neural_all.append(neural_trials)\n            input_all.append(input_trials)\n            output_all_raw.append(me_trials)\n            subject_idx.append(mouse_i)\n            brain_region_idx_all.append(np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=np.int64))\n\n            # Collect ME values for global percentile computation\n            for mt in me_trials:\n                all_me_values.append(mt)\n\n    # Compute global percentile bin edges for motion energy\n    all_me_concat = np.concatenate(all_me_values)\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me_concat, percentiles)\n    print(f\"\\nMotion energy percentile bin edges: {bin_edges}\")\n    print(f\"Motion energy stats: min={all_me_concat.min():.2f}, \"\n          f\"max={all_me_concat.max():.2f}, mean={all_me_concat.mean():.2f}\")\n\n    # Discretize ME into bins (0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1)\n    output_all = []\n    for session_me_trials in output_all_raw:\n        session_output = []\n        for me_trial in session_me_trials:\n            # np.digitize with bin_edges gives 1-based indices, subtract 1 for 0-based\n            binned = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1])  # 0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1\n            session_output.append(binned.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.int64))\n        output_all.append(session_output)\n\n    # Verify bin distribution\n    all_binned = np.concatenate([np.concatenate([t.flatten() for t in s]) for s in output_all])\n    for b in range(N_OUTPUT_BINS):\n        frac = np.mean(all_binned == b)\n        print(f\"  Bin {b}: {frac:.3f} ({frac*100:.1f}%)\")\n\n    # Sanity checks\n    print(f\"\\n{'='*60}\")\n    print(\"SANITY CHECKS\")\n    print(f\"{'='*60}\")\n\n    n_sessions = len(neural_all)\n    print(f\"Total sessions: {n_sessions}\")\n    print(f\"Total mice: {len(subjects)}\")\n\n    neurons_per_mouse = {}\n    sessions_per_mouse = {}\n    for i, mouse_idx in enumerate(subject_idx):\n        mouse = subjects[mouse_idx]\n        n_neur = neural_all[i][0].shape[0]\n        neurons_per_mouse.setdefault(mouse, []).append(n_neur)\n        sessions_per_mouse[mouse] = sessions_per_mouse.get(mouse, 0) + 1\n\n    print(\"\\nNeurons per mouse (should be consistent across sessions):\")\n    for mouse, counts in neurons_per_mouse.items():\n        consistent = \"OK\" if len(set(counts)) == 1 else \"MISMATCH!\"\n        print(f\"  {mouse}: {counts[0]} neurons ({consistent})\")\n\n    all_neuron_counts = [counts[0] for counts in neurons_per_mouse.values()]\n    mean_neurons = np.mean(all_neuron_counts)\n    std_neurons = np.std(all_neuron_counts, ddof=1)\n    print(f\"\\nMean neurons per mouse: {mean_neurons:.0f} +/- {std_neurons:.0f}\")\n    print(f\"Paper reports: 526 +/- 190\")\n\n    print(f\"\\nSessions per mouse:\")\n    for mouse, count in sessions_per_mouse.items():\n        print(f\"  {mouse}: {count}\")\n\n    # Check for NaN/Inf in neural data\n    nan_count = sum(np.any(np.isnan(t)) for s in neural_all for t in s)\n    inf_count = sum(np.any(np.isinf(t)) for s in neural_all for t in s)\n    print(f\"\\nTrials with NaN: {nan_count}, with Inf: {inf_count}\")\n\n    # Build final data dict\n    subject_idx_arr = np.array(subject_idx, dtype=np.int64)\n\n    output_bin_names = [f\"Q{i+1}\" for i in range(N_OUTPUT_BINS)]\n\n    data = {\n        'neural': neural_all,\n        'input': input_all,\n        'output': output_all,\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': subject_idx_arr,\n\n        'brain_regions': ['barrel_cortex'],\n        'brain_region_idx': brain_region_idx_all,\n\n        'input_names': ['time_elapsed_s'],\n        'output_names': ['motion_energy_bin'],\n        'output_values': [output_bin_names],\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description': 'Decode motion energy (5 equal-percentile bins) from barrel cortex calcium imaging during spontaneous behavior in developing mice (P7-P14)',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN_DURATION_MS,\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'Start of recording session',\n            'off_start': 0.0,\n            'off_end': None,\n            'imaging_rate_hz': FS,\n            'bin_size_frames': BIN_SIZE,\n            'trial_duration_sec': TRIAL_DURATION_SEC,\n            'neuropil_coefficient': NEUCOEFF,\n            'baseline_percentile': PRCTILE_BASELINE,\n            'baseline_window_sec': WIN_BASELINE_SEC,\n            'n_output_bins': N_OUTPUT_BINS,\n            'motion_energy_bin_edges': bin_edges.tolist(),\n            'session_info': {\n                mouse: {\n                    'sessions': get_sessions(os.path.join(data_dir, mouse)),\n                    'n_neurons': neurons_per_mouse[mouse][0],\n                    'n_sessions': sessions_per_mouse[mouse]\n                }\n                for mouse in MICE\n            }\n        }\n    }\n\n    # Convert neural data to float32\n    for i in range(len(data['neural'])):\n        data['neural'][i] = [t.astype(np.float32) for t in data['neural'][i]]\n\n    # Save full dataset\n    if not sample_only:\n        print(f\"\\nSaving full dataset to {output_file}...\")\n        with open(output_file, 'wb') as f:\n            pickle.dump(data, f)\n        print(f\"  File size: {os.path.getsize(output_file) / 1e6:.1f} MB\")\n\n    # Create sample dataset (first 2 sessions)\n    n_sample = 2\n    sample_data = {\n        'neural': data['neural'][:n_sample],\n        'input': data['input'][:n_sample],\n        'output': data['output'][:n_sample],\n        'subjects': data['subjects'],\n        'subject_idx': data['subject_idx'][:n_sample],\n        'brain_regions': data['brain_regions'],\n        'brain_region_idx': data['brain_region_idx'][:n_sample],\n        'input_names': data['input_names'],\n        'output_names': data['output_names'],\n        'output_values': data['output_values'],\n        'metadata': data['metadata'],\n    }\n\n    print(f\"Saving sample dataset ({n_sample} sessions) to {sample_output_file}...\")\n    with open(sample_output_file, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(sample_data, f)\n    print(f\"  File size: {os.path.getsize(sample_output_file) / 1e6:.1f} MB\")\n\n    print(f\"\\n{'='*60}\")\n    print(\"Conversion complete!\")\n    print(f\"{'='*60}\")\n\n    return data\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    sample_only = '--sample-only' in sys.argv\n    data = convert_data(sample_only=sample_only)\n","numLines":350,"startLine":1,"totalLines":350}}}

Each mouse has 6-7 sessions recorded at 30 Hz using 2-photon calcium imaging with GCaMP8m.\n\n## Data Loading\n\n### Neural Data\n- **Source**: Suite2p outputs from Track2p's matched-cell pipeline\n  - `F.npy`: Raw fluorescence traces (n_neurons x n_timepoints)\n  - `Fneu.npy`: Neuropil fluorescence\n- **Neurons are pre-matched**: Track2p already identified cells present across all days for each mouse. All neurons in `iscell.npy` are marked as cells (probability >= 0.5 threshold).\n- **No additional neuron filtering** was applied since Track2p already curated the population.\n\n### Behavioral Data\n- **Source**: `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` - global motion energy from videography\n- **Missing video frames**: Detected via `interframe_int.npy` (gaps > 1.5x median interval). Missing frames interpolated linearly. Affects 9/41 sessions (1-148 frames missing).\n\n## Processing Pipeline\n\n### 1. dF/F Computation\nFollowing the paper: \"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\"\n\n- **Neuropil correction**: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu (Suite2p default coefficient)\n- **Baseline**: Suite2p default 'maximin' method:\n  1. Gaussian smooth with sigma = window/6\n  2. Minimum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\n  3. Maximum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\n- **dF/F** = (Fc - F0) / F0\n\n### 2. Temporal Binning\nFollowing the paper: \"we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\"\n\n- Bin size: 10 frames (30 Hz -> 3 Hz, ~333.33 ms per bin)\n- Applied to both neural (dF/F) and behavioral (motion energy) data\n\n### 3. Trial Segmentation\nFollowing the paper: \"splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording\"\n\n- Trial duration: 2 minutes = 360 bins (after 10-frame binning)\n- 20-minute sessions: 10 trials per session\n- 30-minute sessions: 15 trials per session\n- Total: 545 trials across 41 sessions\n\n### 4. Motion Energy Discretization\nFollowing the task specification: \"normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins\"\n\n- Computed global quintile bin edges across all sessions and mice\n- Bin edges: [531661.5, 721126.0, 822566.6, 1015832.6, 1642195.4, 39962858.7]\n- Each bin contains exactly 20% of the data globally\n- Output values: 0-4 (Q1 through Q5)\n\n### 5. Decoder Input\n- **Time elapsed from start of session** (in seconds), time-varying\n- For each trial, time runs from trial_start to trial_end (centers of bins)\n- E.g., trial 0: [0.17, 0.50, ..., 119.83] seconds\n\n## Data Structure\n\n| Field | Value |\n|-------|-------|\n| Sessions | 41 |\n| Subjects | 6 mice |\n| Neurons per mouse | 221 (jm031), 370 (jm032), 685 (jm038), 746 (jm039), 541 (jm040), 435 (jm046) |\n| Mean neurons | 500 +/- 198 (paper: 526 +/- 190) |\n| Trials per session | 10 (20-min) or 15 (30-min) |\n| Timepoints per trial | 360 |\n| Time bin size | 333.33 ms |\n| Brain sessions for each mouse have the same number of neurons (verified).\n2. **Mean neuron count**: 500 +/- 198, consistent with paper's 526 +/- 190.\n3. **Session counts**: 6-7 sessions per mouse, matching paper's \"minimum of 6 consecutive days\".\n4. **Total mice**: 6, matching paper's \"full dataset of 6 mice\".\n5. **No NaN/Inf values**: All trials verified clean.\n6. **Equal-percentile frames).\n\n## Decoder Performance\n\n- **Sample (2 sessions)**: Balanced accuracy = 0.41 (chance = 0.20)\n- **Full dataset**: See train_decoder_full_out.txt\n\n## Decisions and Justifications\n\n1. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/move_deve'):\n    36→- Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')\n    37→- In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera (in case that length of 'motion_energy_glob.npy' doesn't match the number of 2-photon imaging frames)\n    38→- The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\n    39→\n    40→\n    41→## Other data availability:\n    42→\n    43→- Due to space constraints we could not provide the whole raw data\n    44→- Upon reasonable request we can provide:\n    45→    - 'jm031/'):\n- For each subject there is a folder corresponding to the subject id\n- For cross-referencing with the paper the subjects are named in alphabeticaly increasing order (e. g. jm031 - mouse A, jm032 - mouse B ... jm046 - mouse F).\n\nsession sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\n- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\n- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\n- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\n\nsuite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\n\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/move_deve'):\n- Contains the processed behavioural data (motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour 'motion_energy_glob.npy')\n- In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera (in case that length of 'motion_energy_glob.npy' doesn't match the number of 2-photon imaging frames)\n- The indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\n\n\n## Other data availability:\n\n- Due to space constraints we could not provide the whole raw data\n- Upon reasonable request we can provide:\n    - Raw 2-photon imaging data (.tiff files)\n    - Preprocessed Suite2p data for all detected cells on each individual days (not only the ones tracked across all days)\n    - Raw videography data of spontaneous behaviour (.avi files)\n\n","numLines":49,"startLine":1,"totalLines":49}}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ANLcjmLQoqWr11NDToMdWx","type":"tool_result","content":"   440→          \"function_name\": \"Read\",\n   441→          \"arguments\": {\n   442→            \"file_path\": \"/app/methods.txt\"\n   443→          }\n   444→        }\n   445→      ],\n   446→      \"observation\": {\n   447→        \"results\": [\n   448→          {\n   449→            \"source_call_id\": \"toolu_01MPABys1Sm3oR3wvBmcAPaQ\",\n   450→            \"content\": \"1→We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\\n     2→\\n     3→We next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \\n     4→\\n     5→We assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\\n     6→\\n     7→Chronic 2-photon calcium imaging\\n     8→\\n     9→Longitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\\n    10→\\n    11→Videography\\n    12→\\n    13→All videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\\n    14→\\n    15→Processing of calcium imaging data\\n    16→\\n    17→Calcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\\n    18→Preprocessing videography\\n    19→\\n    20→We used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\\n    21→\\n    22→Functional properties of tracked neurons\\n    23→Calcium event rates\\n    24→\\n    25→To quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\\n    26→\\n    27→Decoding\\n    28→\\n    29→All decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\\n    30→\\n    31→L(\\\\beta) = \\\\|y - X\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2 + \\\\lambda\\\\|\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2\\n    32→\\n    33→Where in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\\n    34→\\n\\n[metadata] {\\\"type\\\": \\\"text\\\", \\\"file\\\": {\\\"filePath\\\": \\\"/app/methods.txt\\\", \\\"content\\\": \\\"We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\\n\\nDecoding\\n\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\\n\\nL(\\\\beta) = \\\\|y - X\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2 + \\\\lambda\\\\|\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2\\n\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\\n\",\n   479→              \"numLines\": 34,\n   480→              \"startLine\": 1,\n   481→              \"totalLines\": 34\n   482→            }\n   483→          },\n   484→          \"raw_tool_result\": {\n   485→            \"tool_use_id\": \"toolu_01MPABys1Sm3oR3wvBmcAPaQ\",\n   486→            \"type\": \"tool_result\",\n   487→            \"content\": \"     1→We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\\n     2→\\n     3→We next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \\n     4→\\n     5→We assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\\n     6→\\n     7→Chronic 2-photon calcium imaging\\n     8→\\n     9→Longitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\\n    10→\\n    11→Videography\\n    12→\\n    13→All videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\\n    14→\\n    15→Processing of calcium imaging data\\n    16→\\n    17→Calcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\\n    18→Preprocessing videography\\n    19→\\n    20→We used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\\n    21→\\n    22→Functional properties of tracked neurons\\n    23→Calcium event rates\\n    24→\\n    25→To quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\\n\\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \\n\\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\\n\\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\\n\\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\\n\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\n\\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\\nPreprocessing videography\\n\\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\\n\\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\\nCalcium event rates\\n\\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\\n\\nDecoding\\n\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\\n\\nL(\\\\beta) = \\\\|y - X\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2 + \\\\lambda\\\\|\\\\beta\\\\|_2^2\\n\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\\n\",\n   496→              \"numLines\": 34,\n   497→              \"startLine\": 1,\n   498→              \"totalLines\": 34\n   499→            }\n   500→          },\n   501→          \"raw_tool_result\": {\n   502→            \"tool_use_id\": \"toolu_01MPABys1Sm3oR3wvBmcAPaQ\",\n   503→            \"type\": \"tool_result\",\n   504→            \"content\": \"     1→We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) Let me create a detailed summary for the user:\\\\n\\\\n## Summary: Track2p Code Structure and Processing Pipeline\\\\n\\\\nI've thoroughly explored the /app/code/ directory. Here are my detailed findings:\\\\n\\\\n### **1. TRACK2P OVERVIEW**\\\\nTrack2p is a **longitudinal cell tracking system for calcium imaging recordings** in neuroscience. It automatically matches the same neural cells (ROIs) across multiple consecutive recording days in developing brain tissue (e.g., P8-P14 mice). Published in eLife 2025 (Majnik, Mantez, et al.).\\\\n\\\\n**Key Code Files:**\\\\n- `/app/code/track2p/t2p.py` - Main orchestration pipeline (11 steps)\\\\n- `/app/code/setup.py` - Package configuration\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **2. DATA STRUCTURE AND PROCESSING PIPELINE**\\\\n\\\\nTrack2p processes **suite2p** outputs (standard calcium imaging analysis):\\\\n\\\\n**Input Data:**\\\\n- Multiple datasets, each containing a `suite2p/plane#/` directory with:\\\\n  - `F.npy` - Fluorescence traces for all ROIs\\\\n  - `stat.npy` - ROI spatial properties (xpix, ypix, median coordinates)\\\\n  - `iscell.npy` - Cell classification probabilities\\\\n  - `ops.npy` - Operations metadata (meanImg, nchannels, frame info)\\\\n  - `Fneu.npy` - Neuropil fluorescence\\\\n  - `spks.npy` - Spike inferred activity\\\\n\\\\n**Processing Steps (in t2p.py:run_t2p()):**\\\\n\\\\n1. **Initialize paths** - Create output directories for results and figures\\\\n2. **Load data** - Load all images from all datasets and planes\\\\n3. **Plot available planes** - Visualize functional & anatomical channels\\\\n4. **Select registration channel** - Choose channel 0 (functional) or 1 (anatomical)\\\\n5. **Image registration** - Use Elastix (affine or rigid transforms) to register consecutive dataset pairs\\\\n6. **Transform ROI coordinates** - Apply registration transforms to all cell ROIs\\\\n7. **Compute optimal cell assignments** - Match cells between registered images using:\\\\n   - Cost matrices (IOU, centroid distance, or hybrid methods)\\\\n   - Hungarian assignment algorithm (`scipy.optimize.linear_sum_assignment`)\\\\n   - Otsu/minimum thresholding on IOU scores\\\\n8. **Propagate matches across all days** - Chain pairwise matches into longitudinal tracks\\\\n9. **Save results** - Store match matrices, settings, and suite2p indices\\\\n10. **Generate suite2p format output** - Create filtered datasets containing only matched cells\\\\n11. **Generate visualizations** - Plot registration results and matched ROI overlays\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **3. KEY MODULES**\\\\n\\\\n#### **A. Registration** (`track2p/register/`)\\\\n- **elastix.py** - Wraps ITK (Insight Segmentation and Registration Toolkit) for image registration\\\\n  - `reg_img_elastix()` - Registers moving image to reference\\\\n  - `itk_reg_all_roi()` - Transforms ROI coordinates using computed transforms\\\\n  \\\\n- **loop.py** - Orchestrates registration pipeline\\\\n  - `run_reg_loop()` - Runs registration for all dataset pairs\\\\n  - `reg_all_ds_all_roi()` - Transforms all ROIs using registration parameters\\\\n\\\\n- **utils.py** - Helper functions\\\\n  - `get_all_ds_img_for_reg()` - Selects reference/moving images by channel\\\\n  - `get_all_ref_nonref_inters()` - Computes ROI overlaps for visualization\\\\n\\\\n#### **B. Matching** (`track2p/match/`)\\\\n- **loop.py** - Cell assignment and tracking\\\\n  - `get_all_ds_assign()` - Computes cost matrices and optimal assignments for each pair\\\\n  - `get_all_pl_match_mat()` - Propagates matches forward across all days (tracks individual cells)\\\\n  \\\\n- **utils.py** - Matching algorithms\\\\n  - `get_cost_mat()` - Three matching methods:\\\\n    - `'cent'` - Simple centroid distance\\\\n    - `'cent_int-filt'` - Centroid distance with intersection filtering\\\\n    - `'iou'` - Intersection-over-Union (most accurate, slower)\\\\n  - `get_iou()` - Computes pairwise IOU scores\\\\n  - `get_centroids()` - Computes centroid coordinates for ROIs\\\\n  - `init_all_pl_match_mat()` - Initializes match matrix structure\\\\n\\\\n#### **C. Data I/O** (`track2p/io/`)\\\\n- **loaders.py** - Data loading functions\\\\n  - `load_stat_ds_plane()` - Load and filter ROI statistics by iscell threshold\\\\n  - `get_all_roi_array_from_stat()` - Convert stat to binary ROI arrays\\\\n  \\\\n- **s2p_loaders.py** - Suite2p format loading\\\\n  - `load_all_imgs()` - Load mean images (channel 1 & 2)\\\\n  - `load_all_ds_stat_iscell()` - Load ROI stats with iscell filtering\\\\n  - `load_all_ds_centroids()` - Extract ROI centroids\\\\n  - `load_all_ds_mean_img()` - Load mean images for plotting\\\\n  \\\\n- **savers.py** - Data saving\\\\n  - `save_track_ops()` - Save algorithm parameters\\\\n  - `save_all_pl_match_mat()` - Save match matrices per plane\\\\n  - `npy_to_s2p()` - Convert raw numpy arrays to suite2p format\\\\n\\\\n#### **D. Operations** (`track2p/ops/`)\\\\n- **default.py** - DefaultTrackOps class with all configurable parameters:\\\\n  - `all_ds_path` - Paths to datasets\\\\n  - `reg_chan` - Registration channel (0=functional, 1=anatomical)\\\\n  - `transform_type` - 'affine' or 'rigid'\\\\n  - `iscell_thr` - Suite2p cell classification threshold (default 0.50)\\\\n  - `matching_method` - 'iou', 'cent', or 'cent_int-filt'\\\\n  - `thr_method` - 'otsu' or 'min' for threshold computation\\\\n  - `save_in_s2p_format` - Generate aligned suite2p format output\\\\n\\\\n#### **E. Plotting** (`track2p/plot/`)\\\\n- **output.py** - Visualization functions\\\\n  - `plot_reg_img_output()` - Registration quality before/after\\\\n  - `plot_roi_reg_output()` - ROI transformations\\\\n  - `plot_thr_met_hist()` - Threshold metric distributions\\\\n  - `plot_roi_match_multiplane()` - Individual matched cells across days\\\\n  - `plot_allroi_match_multiplane()` - All matched ROIs projected on mean images\\\\n\\\\n#### **F. Evaluation** (`track2p/eval/`)\\\\n- **io.py** - Load performance metrics (F1 scores, Consistency Tracking)\\\\n  - `load_alldays_ct_values()` - Load CT (Consistency Tracking) metrics\\\\n  - `load_pairwise_f1_values()` - Load pairwise F1 scores\\\\n  \\\\n- **plot.py** - Metric visualization\\\\n  - `plot_alldays_f1()` - F1 score comparison across conditions\\\\n  - `plot_pairwise_f1()` - Temporal tracking accuracy plots\\\\n\\\\n#### **G. GUI** (`track2p/gui/`)\\\\n- Complete PyQt-based GUI for running and visualizing results interactively\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **4. NOTEBOOKS FOR ANALYSIS**\\\\n\\\\n#### **`notebooks/run_t2p.ipynb`**\\\\n- **Purpose**: Template for running track2p programmatically\\\\n- Shows how to:\\\\n  - Import DefaultTrackOps and run_t2p()\\\\n  - Configure parameters (paths, thresholds, registration method)\\\\n  - Execute the full pipeline\\\\n\\\\n#### **`notebooks/demo_t2p_ouputs.ipynb`**\\\\n- **Purpose**: Downstream analysis of track2p results\\\\n- Key workflow:\\\\n  1. Load `plane#_match_mat.npy` (cell identity indices across days)\\\\n  2. Load `track_ops.npy` (pipeline settings)\\\\n  3. Filter to cells present on all days (remove None values)\\\\n  4. Load suite2p data (F traces, stat ROIs) for each day\\\\n  5. Filter by iscell threshold to match track2p filtering\\\\n  6. Index into matched cells using match matrix\\\\n  7. Extract fluorescence traces `all_f_t2p` - same ROI index across all days\\\\n  8. Extract ROI statistics `all_stat_t2p` - spatial properties across days\\\\n  9. Visualize: mean images, matched ROI locations, activity traces, raster plots\\\\n  \\\\n- **Enables downstream analysis**: stability, representational drift, assembly dynamics\\\\n\\\\n#### **`notebooks/eval/eval_t2p.ipynb`**\\\\n- **Purpose**: Evaluation against manual ground truth\\\\n- Workflow:\\\\n  1. Create 8x8 spatial grid on FOV to select cells for manual annotation\\\\n  2. Load track2p match matrix and ground truth manual matches\\\\n  3. Convert to suite2p indices using iscell filtering\\\\n  4. Compare track2p vs. manual vs. CellReg (another tracking tool)\\\\n  5. Compute metrics:\\\\n     - **Tgt**: Number of ground truth tracks\\\\n     - **Tc**: Number of computed tracks\\\\n     - **Trc**: Completely reconstructed tracks\\\\n     - **CT (Consistency Tracking)**: 2*Trc/(Tgt+Tc)\\\\n     - **Accuracy**: Trc/Tgt\\\\n  6. Evaluate separately for cells present on all days\\\\n\\\\n#### **`notebooks/eval/main_eval_fig.ipynb`**\\\\n- **Purpose**: Publication figure generation\\\\n- Loads evaluation metrics from multiple animals/conditions\\\\n- Plots F1/CT scores comparing:\\\\n  - Different registration channels (Anatomical vs. Functional)\\\\n  - Transform types (Affine vs. Rigid)\\\\n  - Competing methods (CellReg)\\\\n- Shows temporal tracking accuracy (pairwise evaluation P8-P14)\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **5. DATA FLOW DIAGRAM**\\\\n\\\\n```\\\\nSuite2p Outputs (Multiple Days)\\\\n├── Day 1: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\\\n├── Day 2: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\\\n└── Day N: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Load Images] → Mean images (Ch1, Ch2)\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Select Channel] → Functional or Anatomical\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Register Pairwise] → Day 1→2, Day 2→3, ..., Day N-1→N\\\\n    ├── Elastix (Affine/Rigid)\\\\n    ├── Generate transforms\\\\n    └── Register moving image to reference\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Transform ROIs] → Apply transforms to all cell coordinates\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Match Cells] → Cost matrix (IOU/Centroid) + Hungarian assignment\\\\n    ├── Threshold filtering (Otsu)\\\\n    └── Get pairwise assignments for each day-pair\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Propagate Forward] → Chain pairwise matches into longitudinal tracks\\\\n    └── For each cell on Day 1: track through Day 2, 3, ..., N\\\\n    ↓\\\\n[Save Outputs]\\\\n├── plane#_match_mat.npy → ROI indices across days\\\\n├── track_ops.npy → Settings and metadata\\\\n├── plane#_suite2p_indices.npy → Final suite2p-compatible indices\\\\n└── Visualizations → Registration & matching quality plots\\\\n```\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **6. NO DECODING-RELATED CODE**\\\\n\\\\n**Important Finding**: There is **NO decoding functionality** in this codebase. Track2p is purely a **longitudinal cell tracking system**. It:\\\\n- Does NOT perform neural decoding\\\\n- Does NOT classify behavioral states\\\\n- Does NOT predict behavior from neural activity\\\\n- Does NOT include machine learning decoders\\\\n\\\\nThe codebase only:\\\\n- Tracks cell identity across time\\\\n- Aligns cell coordinates using image registration\\\\n- Matches cells between days using spatial overlap metrics\\\\n- Outputs matched fluorescence traces for downstream analysis\\\\n\\\\nAny behavioral decoding analysis would need to be performed **externally** on the matched traces and behavioral video data (not included in this repository).\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\n### **7. KEY OUTPUT FILES**\\\\n\\\\nAfter running track2p, the following files are generated in `save_path/track2p/`:\\\\n\\\\n| File | Format | Description |\\\\n|------|--------|-------------|\\\\n| `plane#_match_mat.npy` | (n_cells, n_days) object array | Indices of matched cells; None = lost track |\\\\n| `track_ops.npy` | dict | All pipeline parameters and settings |\\\\n| `plane#_suite2p_indices.npy` | (n_cells, n_days) int | Suite2p-compatible ROI indices |\\\\n| `plane#_suite2p_indices.csv` | CSV | Human-readable match matrix |\\\\n| `fig/reg_img_output.png` | PNG | Registration quality visualization |\\\\n| `fig/roi_reg_output.png` | PNG | ROI coordinate transformation results |\\\\n| `matched_suite2p/` | Directory | Matched cells in suite2p format per day |\\\\n\\\\n---\\\\n\\\\nThis is a **sophisticated neuroimaging analysis tool** designed specifically for longitudinal two-photon calcium imaging in the developing brain, with particular focus on tracking neural population dynamics across weeks of development.\\\"}\\n\\n{\\\"type\\\": \\\"text\\\", \\\"text\\\": \\\"agentId: adb305a6935ed3aa6 (use SendMessage with to: 'adb305a6935ed3aa6' to continue this agent)\\\\n<usage>total_tokens: 60908\\\\ntool_uses: 24\\\\nduration_ms: 52413</usage>\\\"}\\n\\n[metadata] {\\\"status\\\": \\\"completed\\\", \\\"prompt\\\": \\\"Explore the /app/code/ directory thoroughly. Let me create a detailed summary for the user:\\n\\n## Summary: Track2p Code Structure and Processing Pipeline\\n\\nI've thoroughly explored the /app/code/ directory. Here are my detailed findings:\\n\\n### **1. TRACK2P OVERVIEW**\\nTrack2p is a **longitudinal cell tracking system for calcium imaging recordings** in neuroscience. It automatically matches the same neural cells (ROIs) across multiple consecutive recording days in developing brain tissue (e.g., P8-P14 mice). Published in eLife 2025 (Majnik, Mantez, et al.).\\n\\n**Key Code Files:**\\n- `/app/code/track2p/t2p.py` - Main orchestration pipeline (11 steps)\\n- `/app/code/setup.py` - Package configuration\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **2. DATA STRUCTURE AND PROCESSING PIPELINE**\\n\\nTrack2p processes **suite2p** outputs (standard calcium imaging analysis):\\n\\n**Input Data:**\\n- Multiple datasets, each containing a `suite2p/plane#/` directory with:\\n  - `F.npy` - Fluorescence traces for all ROIs\\n  - `stat.npy` - ROI spatial properties (xpix, ypix, median coordinates)\\n  - `iscell.npy` - Cell classification probabilities\\n  - `ops.npy` - Operations metadata (meanImg, nchannels, frame info)\\n  - `Fneu.npy` - Neuropil fluorescence\\n  - `spks.npy` - Spike inferred activity\\n\\n**Processing Steps (in t2p.py:run_t2p()):**\\n\\n1. **Initialize paths** - Create output directories for results and figures\\n2. **Load data** - Load all images from all datasets and planes\\n3. **Plot available planes** - Visualize functional & anatomical channels\\n4. **Select registration channel** - Choose channel 0 (functional) or 1 (anatomical)\\n5. **Image registration** - Use Elastix (affine or rigid transforms) to register consecutive dataset pairs\\n6. **Transform ROI coordinates** - Apply registration transforms to all cell ROIs\\n7. **Compute optimal cell assignments** - Match cells between registered images using:\\n   - Cost matrices (IOU, centroid distance, or hybrid methods)\\n   - Hungarian assignment algorithm (`scipy.optimize.linear_sum_assignment`)\\n   - Otsu/minimum thresholding on IOU scores\\n8. **Propagate matches across all days** - Chain pairwise matches into longitudinal tracks\\n9. **Save results** - Store match matrices, settings, and suite2p indices\\n10. **Generate suite2p format output** - Create filtered datasets containing only matched cells\\n11. **Generate visualizations** - Plot registration results and matched ROI overlays\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **3. KEY MODULES**\\n\\n#### **A. Registration** (`track2p/register/`)\\n- **elastix.py** - Wraps ITK (Insight Segmentation and Registration Toolkit) for image registration\\n  - `reg_img_elastix()` - Registers moving image to reference\\n  - `itk_reg_all_roi()` - Transforms ROI coordinates using computed transforms\\n  \\n- **loop.py** - Orchestrates registration pipeline\\n  - `run_reg_loop()` - Runs registration for all dataset pairs\\n  - `reg_all_ds_all_roi()` - Transforms all ROIs using registration parameters\\n\\n- **utils.py** - Helper functions\\n  - `get_all_ds_img_for_reg()` - Selects reference/moving images by channel\\n  - `get_all_ref_nonref_inters()` - Computes ROI overlaps for visualization\\n\\n#### **B. Matching** (`track2p/match/`)\\n- **loop.py** - Cell assignment and tracking\\n  - `get_all_ds_assign()` - Computes cost matrices and optimal assignments for each pair\\n  - `get_all_pl_match_mat()` - Propagates matches forward across all days (tracks individual cells)\\n  \\n- **utils.py** - Matching algorithms\\n  - `get_cost_mat()` - Three matching methods:\\n    - `'cent'` - Simple centroid distance\\n    - `'cent_int-filt'` - Centroid distance with intersection filtering\\n    - `'iou'` - Intersection-over-Union (most accurate, slower)\\n  - `get_iou()` - Computes pairwise IOU scores\\n  - `get_centroids()` - Computes centroid coordinates for ROIs\\n  - `init_all_pl_match_mat()` - Initializes match matrix structure\\n\\n#### **C. Data I/O** (`track2p/io/`)\\n- **loaders.py** - Data loading functions\\n  - `load_stat_ds_plane()` - Load and filter ROI statistics by iscell threshold\\n  - `get_all_roi_array_from_stat()` - Convert stat to binary ROI arrays\\n  \\n- **s2p_loaders.py** - Suite2p format loading\\n  - `load_all_imgs()` - Load mean images (channel 1 & 2)\\n  - `load_all_ds_stat_iscell()` - Load ROI stats with iscell filtering\\n  - `load_all_ds_centroids()` - Extract ROI centroids\\n  - `load_all_ds_mean_img()` - Load mean images for plotting\\n  \\n- **savers.py** - Data saving\\n  - `save_track_ops()` - Save algorithm parameters\\n  - `save_all_pl_match_mat()` - Save match matrices per plane\\n  - `npy_to_s2p()` - Convert raw numpy arrays to suite2p format\\n\\n#### **D. Operations** (`track2p/ops/`)\\n- **default.py** - DefaultTrackOps class with all configurable parameters:\\n  - `all_ds_path` - Paths to datasets\\n  - `reg_chan` - Registration channel (0=functional, 1=anatomical)\\n  - `transform_type` - 'affine' or 'rigid'\\n  - `iscell_thr` - Suite2p cell classification threshold (default 0.50)\\n  - `matching_method` - 'iou', 'cent', or 'cent_int-filt'\\n  - `thr_method` - 'otsu' or 'min' for threshold computation\\n  - `save_in_s2p_format` - Generate aligned suite2p format output\\n\\n#### **E. Plotting** (`track2p/plot/`)\\n- **output.py** - Visualization functions\\n  - `plot_reg_img_output()` - Registration quality before/after\\n  - `plot_roi_reg_output()` - ROI transformations\\n  - `plot_thr_met_hist()` - Threshold metric distributions\\n  - `plot_roi_match_multiplane()` - Individual matched cells across days\\n  - `plot_allroi_match_multiplane()` - All matched ROIs projected on mean images\\n\\n#### **F. Evaluation** (`track2p/eval/`)\\n- **io.py** - Load performance metrics (F1 scores, Consistency Tracking)\\n  - `load_alldays_ct_values()` - Load CT (Consistency Tracking) metrics\\n  - `load_pairwise_f1_values()` - Load pairwise F1 scores\\n  \\n- **plot.py** - Metric visualization\\n  - `plot_alldays_f1()` - F1 score comparison across conditions\\n  - `plot_pairwise_f1()` - Temporal tracking accuracy plots\\n\\n#### **G. GUI** (`track2p/gui/`)\\n- Complete PyQt-based GUI for running and visualizing results interactively\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **4. NOTEBOOKS FOR ANALYSIS**\\n\\n#### **`notebooks/run_t2p.ipynb`**\\n- **Purpose**: Template for running track2p programmatically\\n- Shows how to:\\n  - Import DefaultTrackOps and run_t2p()\\n  - Configure parameters (paths, thresholds, registration method)\\n  - Execute the full pipeline\\n\\n#### **`notebooks/demo_t2p_ouputs.ipynb`**\\n- **Purpose**: Downstream analysis of track2p results\\n- Key workflow:\\n  1. Load `plane#_match_mat.npy` (cell identity indices across days)\\n  2. Load `track_ops.npy` (pipeline settings)\\n  3. Filter to cells present on all days (remove None values)\\n  4. Load suite2p data (F traces, stat ROIs) for each day\\n  5. Filter by iscell threshold to match track2p filtering\\n  6. Index into matched cells using match matrix\\n  7. Extract fluorescence traces `all_f_t2p` - same ROI index across all days\\n  8. Extract ROI statistics `all_stat_t2p` - spatial properties across days\\n  9. Visualize: mean images, matched ROI locations, activity traces, raster plots\\n  \\n- **Enables downstream analysis**: stability, representational drift, assembly dynamics\\n\\n#### **`notebooks/eval/eval_t2p.ipynb`**\\n- **Purpose**: Evaluation against manual ground truth\\n- Workflow:\\n  1. Create 8x8 spatial grid on FOV to select cells for manual annotation\\n  2. Load track2p match matrix and ground truth manual matches\\n  3. Convert to suite2p indices using iscell filtering\\n  4. Compare track2p vs. manual vs. CellReg (another tracking tool)\\n  5. Compute metrics:\\n     - **Tgt**: Number of ground truth tracks\\n     - **Tc**: Number of computed tracks\\n     - **Trc**: Completely reconstructed tracks\\n     - **CT (Consistency Tracking)**: 2*Trc/(Tgt+Tc)\\n     - **Accuracy**: Trc/Tgt\\n  6. Evaluate separately for cells present on all days\\n\\n#### **`notebooks/eval/main_eval_fig.ipynb`**\\n- **Purpose**: Publication figure generation\\n- Loads evaluation metrics from multiple animals/conditions\\n- Plots F1/CT scores comparing:\\n  - Different registration channels (Anatomical vs. Functional)\\n  - Transform types (Affine vs. Rigid)\\n  - Competing methods (CellReg)\\n- Shows temporal tracking accuracy (pairwise evaluation P8-P14)\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **5. Create 8x8 spatial grid on FOV to select cells for manual annotation\\n  2. Load track2p match matrix and ground truth manual matches\\n  3. Convert to suite2p indices using iscell filtering\\n  4. Compare track2p vs. manual vs. CellReg (another tracking tool)\\n  5. Compute metrics:\\n     - **Tgt**: Number of ground truth tracks\\n     - **Tc**: Number of computed tracks\\n     - **Trc**: Completely reconstructed tracks\\n     - **CT (Consistency Tracking)**: 2*Trc/(Tgt+Tc)\\n     - **Accuracy**: Trc/Tgt\\n  6. Evaluate separately for cells present on all days\\n\\n#### **`notebooks/eval/main_eval_fig.ipynb`**\\n- **Purpose**: Publication figure generation\\n- Loads evaluation metrics from multiple animals/conditions\\n- Plots F1/CT scores comparing:\\n  - Different registration channels (Anatomical vs. Functional)\\n  - Transform types (Affine vs. Rigid)\\n  - Competing methods (CellReg)\\n- Shows temporal tracking accuracy (pairwise evaluation P8-P14)\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **5. DATA FLOW DIAGRAM**\\n\\n```\\nSuite2p Outputs (Multiple Days)\\n├── Day 1: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\n├── Day 2: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\n└── Day N: F.npy, stat.npy, iscell.npy, ops.npy\\n    ↓\\n[Load Images] → Mean images (Ch1, Ch2)\\n    ↓\\n[Select Channel] → Functional or Anatomical\\n    ↓\\n[Register Pairwise] → Day 1→2, Day 2→3, ..., Day N-1→N\\n    ├── Elastix (Affine/Rigid)\\n    ├── Generate transforms\\n    └── Register moving image to reference\\n    ↓\\n[Transform ROIs] → Apply transforms to all cell coordinates\\n    ↓\\n[Match Cells] → Cost matrix (IOU/Centroid) + Hungarian assignment\\n    ├── Threshold filtering (Otsu)\\n    └── Get pairwise assignments for each day-pair\\n    ↓\\n[Propagate Forward] → Chain pairwise matches into longitudinal tracks\\n    └── For each cell on Day 1: track through Day 2, 3, ..., N\\n    ↓\\n[Save Outputs]\\n├── plane#_match_mat.npy → ROI indices across days\\n├── track_ops.npy → Settings and metadata\\n├── plane#_suite2p_indices.npy → Final suite2p-compatible indices\\n└── Visualizations → Registration & matching quality plots\\n```\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **6. NO DECODING-RELATED CODE**\\n\\n**Important Finding**: There is **NO decoding functionality** in this codebase. Track2p is purely a **longitudinal cell tracking system**. It:\\n- Does NOT perform neural decoding\\n- Does NOT classify behavioral states\\n- Does NOT predict behavior from neural activity\\n- Does NOT include machine learning decoders\\n\\nThe codebase only:\\n- Tracks cell identity across time\\n- Aligns cell coordinates using image registration\\n- Matches cells between days using spatial overlap metrics\\n- Outputs matched fluorescence traces for downstream analysis\\n\\nAny behavioral decoding analysis would need to be performed **externally** on the matched traces and behavioral video data (not included in this repository).\\n\\n---\\n\\n### **7. KEY OUTPUT FILES**\\n\\nAfter running track2p, the following files are generated in `save_path/track2p/`:\\n\\n| File | Format | Description |\\n|------|--------|-------------|\\n| `plane#_match_mat.npy` | (n_cells, n_days) object array | Indices of matched cells; None = lost track |\\n| `track_ops.npy` | dict | All pipeline parameters and settings |\\n| `plane#_suite2p_indices.npy` | (n_cells, n_days) int | Suite2p-compatible ROI indices |\\n| `plane#_suite2p_indices.csv` | CSV | Human-readable match matrix |\\n| `fig/reg_img_output.png` | PNG | Registration quality visualization |\\n| `fig/roi_reg_output.png` | PNG | ROI coordinate transformation results |\\n| `matched_suite2p/` | Directory | Matched cells in suite2p format per day |\\n\\n---\\n\\nThis is a **sophisticated neuroimaging analysis tool** designed specifically for longitudinal two-photon calcium imaging in the developing brain, with particular focus on tracking neural population dynamics across weeks of development.\"\n  1528→              },\n  1529→              {\n  1530→                \"type\": \"text\",\n  1531→                \"text\": \"agentId: adb305a6935ed3aa6 (use SendMessage with to: 'adb305a6935ed3aa6' to continue this agent)\\n<usage>total_tokens: 60908\\ntool_uses: 24\\nduration_ms: 52413</usage>\"\n  1532→              }\n  1533→            ]\n  1534→          }"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01EABUs1UMuB9vEredP2acUK","session_id":"7a31ff73-14c4-4dca-ae95-8341d07f235d","uuid":"40c939bf-364a-4d28-9353-26583f43b836","timestamp":"2026-08-03T14:16:50.089Z"}

\\\"\\n   204→          f\\\"max={all_me_concat.max():.2f}, mean={all_me_concat.mean():.2f}\\\")\\n   205→\\n   206→    # Discretize ME into bins (0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1)\\n   207→    output_all = []\\n    print(f\\\"  {mouse}: {counts[0]} neurons ({consistent})\\\")\\n   243→\\n   244→    all_neuron_counts = [counts[0] for counts in neurons_per_mouse.values()]\\n   245→    mean_neurons =
