'--sample-only' in sys.argv\\n   345→    data = convert_data(sample_only=sample_only)\\n   346→\\n\\n[metadata] {\\\"type\\\": \\\"text\\\", \\\"file\\\": {\\\"filePath\\\": \\\"/app/convert_data.py\\\", \\\"content\\\": \\\"#!/usr/bin/env python3\\\\n\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\nConvert Track2p longitudinal calcium imaging data to decoder format.\\\\n\\\\nReference: Majnik et al. 2025, \\\\\\\"Longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from\\\\nthe same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\\\\\\\", eLife.\\\\n\\\\nData: 6 mice imaged daily in barrel cortex (layer 2/3) during P7-P14.\\\\nNeural: dF/F computed from Suite2p outputs (F, Fneu) with standard baseline correction.\\\\nBehavioral: Motion energy from videography, normalized and discretized into 5 bins.\\\\n\\\\nProcessing follows the paper's methods:\\\\n- dF/F: neuropil-corrected (F - 0.7*Fneu), baseline = 8th percentile over 60s window\\\\n- Binning: average over 10 consecutive frames (30Hz -> 3Hz, ~333ms bins)\\\\n- Trials: consecutive 2-minute blocks of the recording\\\\n- Motion energy: squared pixel-wise frame differences, normalized, 5 equal-percentile bins\\\\n\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\nimport os\\\\nimport sys\\\\nimport numpy as np\\\\nimport pickle\\\\nfrom scipy.ndimage import percentile_filter\\\\n\\\\n# ============ PARAMETERS ============\\\\nDATA_DIR = 'data'\\\\nOUTPUT_FILE = 'converted_data.pkl'\\\\nSAMPLE_OUTPUT_FILE = 'sample_data.pkl'\\\\n\\\\nMICE = ['jm031', 'jm032', 'jm038', 'jm039', 'jm040', 'jm046']\\\\n\\\\nBIN_SIZE = 10           # temporal binning factor (10 frames averaged)\\\\nFS = 30                 # imaging frame rate (Hz)\\\\nNEUCOEFF = 0.7          # neuropil correction coefficient (Suite2p default)\\\\nWIN_BASELINE_SEC = 60   # baseline window in seconds\\\\nPRCTILE_BASELINE = 8    # baseline percentile (Suite2p default)\\\\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 120  # 2-minute trial blocks (as in paper's decoding)\\\\nN_OUTPUT_BINS = 5       # number of motion energy bins (quintiles)\\\\n\\\\n# Derived\\\\nBIN_DURATION_MS = (BIN_SIZE / FS) * 1000  # ~333.33 ms\\\\nTRIAL_BINS = int(TRIAL_DURATION_SEC * FS / BIN_SIZE)  # 360 bins per trial\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef get_sessions(mouse_dir):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Get sorted session directories for a mouse.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    sessions = [d for d in os.listdir(mouse_dir)\\\\n                if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(mouse_dir, d)) and d[0] == '2']\\\\n    sessions.sort()\\\\n    return sessions\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    Compute dF/F using Suite2p default parameters.\\\\n    1. Neuropil correction: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu\\\\n    2. Baseline: 8th percentile over 60-second sliding window\\\\n    3. dF/F = (Fc - F0) / F0\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    Fc = F - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\\\\n    win = int(WIN_BASELINE_SEC * FS)\\\\n    F0 = percentile_filter(Fc, PRCTILE_BASELINE, size=(1, win))\\\\n    # Avoid division by zero/near-zero\\\\n    F0_safe = np.where(np.abs(F0) < 1e-6, 1e-6, F0)\\\\n    dff = (Fc - F0) / F0_safe\\\\n    return dff\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef align_motion_energy(me, interframe_int, n_neural_frames):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    Align motion energy to neural frames by detecting and interpolating dropped video frames.\\\\n    Uses interframe_int to detect gaps (> 1.5x median interval = dropped frame).\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\\\\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\\\\n\\\\n    median_ifi = np.median(interframe_int)\\\\n\\\\n    # Build aligned array by inserting NaN at dropped frame locations\\\\n    aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\\\\n    neural_idx = 0\\\\n    for i in range(len(me)):\\\\n        if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\\\\n            aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\\\\n        neural_idx += 1\\\\n        # Check if next interval indicates dropped frames\\\\n        if i < len(interframe_int):\\\\n            n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\\\\n            neural_idx += n_dropped\\\\n\\\\n    # Interpolate NaN values\\\\n    nans = np.isnan(aligned)\\\\n    if np.any(nans) and not np.all(nans):\\\\n        x = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\\\\n        aligned[nans] = np.interp(x[nans], x[~nans], aligned[~nans])\\\\n\\\\n    return aligned\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef bin_array(data, bin_size):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Average data over consecutive bins along the last axis.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    if data.ndim == 1:\\\\n        n_bins = len(data) // bin_size\\\\n        return data[:n_bins * bin_size].reshape(n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=1)\\\\n    else:\\\\n        n_bins = data.shape[1] // bin_size\\\\n        return data[:, :n_bins * bin_size].reshape(data.shape[0], n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=2)\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef split_into_trials(data, trial_length):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Split data into non-overlapping trials of fixed length along last axis.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    if data.ndim == 1:\\\\n        n_trials = len(data) // trial_length\\\\n        return [data[i*trial_length:(i+1)*trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\\\\n    else:\\\\n        n_trials = data.shape[1] // trial_length\\\\n        return [data[:, i*trial_length:(i+1)*trial_length] for i in range(n_trials)]\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef convert_data(data_dir=DATA_DIR, output_file=OUTPUT_FILE, sample_output_file=SAMPLE_OUTPUT_FILE,\\\\n                 sample_only=False):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Main conversion function.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n    print(\\\\\\\"=\\\\\\\" * 60)\\\\n    print(\\\\\\\"Converting Track2p data to decoder format\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(\\\\\\\"=\\\\\\\" * 60)\\\\n\\\\n    neural_all = []\\\\n    input_all = []\\\\n    output_all_raw = []  # store raw ME values before discretization\\\\n    subjects = MICE[:]\\\\n    subject_idx = []\\\\n    brain_region_idx_all = []\\\\n\\\\n    # First pass: load, process, bin all data\\\\n    all_me_values = []  # collect all binned ME for global percentile computation\\\\n\\\\n    for mouse_i, mouse in enumerate(MICE):\\\\n        mouse_dir = os.path.join(data_dir, mouse)\\\\n        sessions = get_sessions(mouse_dir)\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\n--- Mouse {mouse} ({len(sessions)} sessions) ---\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        for sess_name in sessions:\\\\n            sess_dir = os.path.join(mouse_dir, sess_name)\\\\n            suite2p_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\\\\n            move_dir = os.path.join(sess_dir, 'move_deve')\\\\n\\\\n            # Load neural data\\\\n            F = np.load(os.path.join(suite2p_dir, 'F.npy'))\\\\n            Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(suite2p_dir, 'Fneu.npy'))\\\\n            n_neurons, n_frames = F.shape\\\\n\\\\n            # Compute dF/F\\\\n            dff = compute_dff(F, Fneu)\\\\n\\\\n            # Load behavioral data\\\\n            me_raw = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\\\\n            ifi = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'interframe_int.npy'))\\\\n\\\\n            # Align ME to neural frames (handle dropped video frames)\\\\n            me_aligned = align_motion_energy(me_raw, ifi, n_frames)\\\\n\\\\n            # Bin both neural and ME by BIN_SIZE frames\\\\n            dff_binned = bin_array(dff, BIN_SIZE)\\\\n            me_binned = bin_array(me_aligned, BIN_SIZE)\\\\n\\\\n            n_bins = dff_binned.shape[1]\\\\n            n_trials = n_bins // TRIAL_BINS\\\\n\\\\n            print(f\\\\\\\"  {sess_name}: {n_neurons} neurons, {n_frames} frames -> \\\\\\\"\\\\n                  f\\\\\\\"{n_bins} bins -> {n_trials} trials of {TRIAL_BINS} bins\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n            # Split into trials\\\\n            neural_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, TRIAL_BINS)\\\\n            me_trials = split_into_trials(me_binned, TRIAL_BINS)\\\\n\\\\n            # Create time input for each trial (time elapsed from start of session, in seconds)\\\\n            input_trials = []\\\\n            for t_i in range(n_trials):\\\\n                start_sec = t_i * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\\\\n                time_bins = np.linspace(\\\\n                    start_sec + BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,  # center of first bin\\\\n                    start_sec + TRIAL_DURATION_SEC - BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,  # center of last bin\\\\n                    TRIAL_BINS\\\\n                )\\\\n                input_trials.append(time_bins.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.float32))\\\\n\\\\n            neural_all.append(neural_trials)\\\\n            input_all.append(input_trials)\\\\n            output_all_raw.append(me_trials)\\\\n            subject_idx.append(mouse_i)\\\\n            brain_region_idx_all.append(np.zeros(n_neurons, dtype=np.int64))\\\\n\\\\n            # Collect ME values for global percentile computation\\\\n            for mt in me_trials:\\\\n                all_me_values.append(mt)\\\\n\\\\n    # Compute global percentile bin edges for motion energy\\\\n    all_me_concat = np.concatenate(all_me_values)\\\\n    percentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)\\\\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(all_me_concat, percentiles)\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nMotion energy percentile bin edges: {bin_edges}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Motion energy stats: min={all_me_concat.min():.2f}, \\\\\\\"\\\\n          f\\\\\\\"max={all_me_concat.max():.2f}, mean={all_me_concat.mean():.2f}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Discretize ME into bins (0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1)\\\\n    output_all = []\\\\n    for session_me_trials in output_all_raw:\\\\n        session_output = []\\\\n        for me_trial in session_me_trials:\\\\n            # np.digitize with bin_edges gives 1-based indices, subtract 1 for 0-based\\\\n            binned = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1])  # 0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1\\\\n            session_output.append(binned.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.int64))\\\\n        output_all.append(session_output)\\\\n\\\\n    # Verify bin distribution\\\\n    all_binned = np.concatenate([np.concatenate([t.flatten() for t in s]) for s in output_all])\\\\n    for b in range(N_OUTPUT_BINS):\\\\n        frac = np.mean(all_binned == b)\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"  Bin {b}: {frac:.3f} ({frac*100:.1f}%)\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Sanity checks\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\n{'='*60}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(\\\\\\\"SANITY CHECKS\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"{'='*60}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    n_sessions = len(neural_all)\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Total sessions: {n_sessions}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Total mice: {len(subjects)}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    neurons_per_mouse = {}\\\\n    sessions_per_mouse = {}\\\\n    for i, mouse_idx in enumerate(subject_idx):\\\\n        mouse = subjects[mouse_idx]\\\\n        n_neur = neural_all[i][0].shape[0]\\\\n        neurons_per_mouse.setdefault(mouse, []).append(n_neur)\\\\n        sessions_per_mouse[mouse] = sessions_per_mouse.get(mouse, 0) + 1\\\\n\\\\n    print(\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nNeurons per mouse (should be consistent across sessions):\\\\\\\")\\\\n    for mouse, counts in neurons_per_mouse.items():\\\\n        consistent = \\\\\\\"OK\\\\\\\" if len(set(counts)) == 1 else \\\\\\\"MISMATCH!\\\\\\\"\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"  {mouse}: {counts[0]} neurons ({consistent})\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    all_neuron_counts = [counts[0] for counts in neurons_per_mouse.values()]\\\\n    mean_neurons = np.mean(all_neuron_counts)\\\\n    std_neurons = np.std(all_neuron_counts, ddof=1)\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nMean neurons per mouse: {mean_neurons:.0f} +/- {std_neurons:.0f}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Paper reports: 526 +/- 190\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nSessions per mouse:\\\\\\\")\\\\n    for mouse, count in sessions_per_mouse.items():\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"  {mouse}: {count}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # \"originalFile\": \"#!/usr/bin/env python3\\n\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nConvert Track2p longitudinal calcium imaging data to decoder format.\\n\\nReference: Majnik et al. 2025, \\\"Longitudinal tracking of (Suite2p default)\\nTRIAL_DURATION_SEC = 120  # 2-minute trial blocks (as in paper's decoding)\\nN_OUTPUT_BINS = 5       # number of motion energy bins (quintiles)\\n\\n# Derived\\nBIN_DURATION_MS = for a mouse.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    sessions = [d for d in os.listdir(mouse_dir)\\n                if os.path.isdir(os.path.join(mouse_dir, d)) and d[0] == '2']\\n    sessions.sort()\\n    return sessions\\n\\n\\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Compute dF/F using Suite2p default parameters.\\n    1. Neuropil correction: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu\\n    2. Baseline: Suite2p default 'maximin' method\\n       - Gaussian smooth, then min filter, then max filter over 60s window\\n    3. dF/F = (Fc - F0) / F0\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Fc = F - NEUCOEFF * Fneu\\n    win = int(WIN_BASELINE_SEC * FS)\\n    # Suite2p default 'maximin' baseline\\n    Flow = gaussian_filter1d(Fc.astype(np.float64), sigma=win / 6, axis=1)\\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow,   return dff\\n\\n\\ndef align_motion_energy(me, interframe_int, n_neural_frames):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    Align motion energy to neural frames by detecting and interpolating dropped video frames.\\n    Uses interframe_int to detect gaps (> 1.5x median interval = dropped frame).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\\n\\n    median_ifi = np.median(interframe_int)\\n\\n    # Build aligned array by inserting NaN at dropped frame locations\\n    aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\\n    neural_idx = 0\\n    for i in range(len(me)):\\n        if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\\n            aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\\n        neural_idx += 1\\n        # Check if next interval indicates dropped frames\\n        if i < len(interframe_int):\\n            n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\\n            neural_idx += n_dropped\\n\\n    # Interpolate NaN values\\n    nans = bin_size].reshape(data.shape[0], n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=2)\\n\\n\\ndef split_into_trials(data, trial_length):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Split data into non-overlapping trials of fixed length along last parameters.\\n    1. Each mouse has 6-7 sessions recorded at 30 Hz using 2-photon calcium imaging with GCaMP8m.\\n\\n## Data Loading\\n\\n### Neural Data\\n- **Source**: Suite2p outputs from Track2p's matched-cell pipeline\\n  - `F.npy`: Raw fluorescence traces (n_neurons x n_timepoints)\\n  - `Fneu.npy`: Neuropil fluorescence\\n- **Neurons are pre-matched**: Track2p already identified cells present across all days for each mouse. All neurons in `iscell.npy` are marked as cells (probability >= 0.5 threshold).\\n- **No additional neuron filtering** was applied since Track2p already curated the population.\\n\\n### Behavioral Data\\n- **Source**: `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` - global motion energy from videography\\n- **Missing video frames**: Detected via `interframe_int.npy` (gaps > 1.5x median interval). Missing frames interpolated linearly. Affects 9/41 sessions (1-148 frames missing).\\n\\n## Processing Pipeline\\n\\n### 1. dF/F Computation\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\\\"\\n\\n- **Neuropil correction**: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu (Suite2p default coefficient)\\n- **Baseline**: Suite2p default 'maximin' method:\\n  1. Gaussian smooth with sigma = window/6\\n  2. Minimum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\\n  3. Maximum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\\n- **dF/F** = (Fc - F0) / F0\\n\\n### 2. Temporal Binning\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\\\"\\n\\n- Bin size: 10 frames (30 Hz -> 3 Hz, ~333.33 ms per bin)\\n- Applied to both neural (dF/F) and behavioral (motion energy) data\\n\\n### 3. Trial Segmentation\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording\\\"\\n\\n- Trial duration: 2 minutes = 360 bins (after 10-frame binning)\\n- 20-minute sessions: 10 trials per session\\n- 30-minute sessions: 15 trials per session\\n- Total: 545 trials across 41 sessions\\n\\n### 4. Motion Energy Discretization\\nFollowing the task specification: \\\"normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins\\\"\\n\\n- Computed global quintile bin edges across all sessions and mice\\n- Bin edges: [531661.5, 721126.0, 822566.6, 1015832.6, 1642195.4, 39962858.7]\\n- Each bin contains exactly 20% of the data globally\\n- Output values: 0-4 (Q1 through Q5)\\n\\n### 5. Decoder Input\\n- **Time elapsed from start of session** (in seconds), time-varying\\n- For each trial, time runs from trial_start to trial_end (centers of bins)\\n- E.g., trial 0: [0.17, 0.50, ..., 119.83] seconds\\n\\n## Data Structure\\n\\n| Field | Value |\\n|-------|-------|\\n| Sessions | 41 |\\n| Subjects | 6 mice |\\n| Neurons per mouse | 221 (jm031), 370 (jm032), 685 (jm038), 746 (jm039), 541 (jm040), 435 (jm046) |\\n| Mean neurons | 500 +/- 198 (paper: 526 +/- 190) |\\n| Trials per session | 10 (20-min) or 15 (30-min) |\\n| Timepoints per trial | 360 |\\n| Time bin size | 333.33 ms |\\n| Brain region | barrel_cortex |\\n| Input dimension | 1 (time_elapsed_s) |\\n| Output dimension | 1 (motion_energy_bin, 5 classes) |\\n\\n## Sanity Checks\\n\\n1. **Neuron counts match across sessions**: All sessions for each mouse have the same number of neurons (verified).\\n2. **Mean neuron count**: 500 +/- 198, consistent with paper's 526 +/- 190.\\n3. **Session counts**: 6-7 sessions per mouse, matching paper's \\\"minimum of 6 consecutive days\\\".\\n4. **Total mice**: 6, matching paper's \\\"full dataset of 6 mice\\\".\\n5. **No NaN/Inf values**: All trials verified clean.\\n6. **Equal-percentile bins**: Each bin contains exactly 20% of global data.\\n7. **Frame rate**: 30 Hz confirmed from Suite2p ops.npy.\\n8. **Recording durations**: jm031/jm032 = 20 min Motion energy discretized using global quintiles to ensure balanced classes across the full dataset.\\n3. **Maximin baseline**: Used Suite2p's default 'maximin' method for dF/F baseline, as the paper states \\\"default Suite2p parameters.\\\"\\n4. **Linear interpolation for missing frames**: Simple and appropriate for small gaps (1-3 frames typical). Affects 9/41 sessions (1-148 frames missing).\\n\\n## Processing Pipeline\\n\\n### 1. dF/F Computation\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\\\"\\n\\n- **Neuropil correction**: Fc = F - 0.7 * Fneu (Suite2p default coefficient)\\n- **Baseline**: Suite2p default 'maximin' method:\\n  1. Gaussian smooth with sigma = window/6\\n  2. Minimum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\\n  3. Maximum filter (60-second window = 1800 frames)\\n- **dF/F** = (Fc - F0) / F0\\n\\n### 2. Temporal Binning\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\\\"\\n\\n- Bin size: 10 frames (30 Hz -> 3 Hz, ~333.33 ms per bin)\\n- Applied to both neural (dF/F) and behavioral (motion energy) data\\n\\n### 3. Trial Segmentation\\nFollowing the paper: \\\"splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording\\\"\\n\\n- Trial duration: 2 minutes = 360 bins (after 10-frame binning)\\n- 20-minute sessions: 10 trials per session\\n- 30-minute sessions: 15 trials per session\\n- Total: 545 trials across 41 sessions\\n\\n### 4. Motion Energy Discretization\\nFollowing the task specification: \\\"normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins\\\"\\n\\n- Computed global quintile bin edges across all sessions and mice\\n- Bin edges: [531661.5, 721126.0, 822566.6, 1015832.6, 1642195.4, 39962858.7]\\n- Each bin contains exactly 20% of the data globally\\n- Output values: 0-4 (Q1 through Q5)\\n\\n### 5. Decoder Input\\n- **Time elapsed from start of session** (in seconds), time-varying\\n- For each trial, time runs from trial_start to trial_end (centers of bins)\\n- E.g., trial 0: [0.17, 0.50, ..., 119.83] seconds\\n\\n## Data Structure\\n\\n| Field | Value |\\n|-------|-------|\\n| Sessions | 41 |\\n| Subjects | 6 mice |\\n| Neurons per mouse | 221 (jm031), 370 (jm032), 685 (jm038), 746 (jm039), 541 (jm040), 435 (jm046) |\\n| Mean neurons | 500 +/- 198 (paper: 526 +/- 190) |\\n| Trials per session | 10 (20-min) or 15 (30-min) |\\n| Timepoints per trial | 360 |\\n| Time bin size | 333.33 ms |\\n| Brain region | barrel_cortex |\\n| Input dimension | 1 (time_elapsed_s) |\\n| Output dimension | 1 (motion_energy_bin, 5 classes) |\\n\\n## Sanity Checks\\n\\n1. **Neuron counts match across sessions**: All sessions for each mouse have the same number of neurons (verified).\\n2. **Mean neuron count**: 500 +/- 198, consistent with paper's 526 +/- 190.\\n3. **Session counts**: 6-7 sessions per mouse, matching paper's \\\"minimum of 6 consecutive days\\\".\\n4. **Total mice**: 6, matching paper's \\\"full dataset of 6 mice\\\".\\n5. **No NaN/Inf values**: All trials verified clean.\\n6. **Equal-percentile bins**: Each bin contains exactly 20% of global data.\\n7. **Frame rate**: 30 Hz confirmed from Suite2p ops.npy.\\n8. **Recording durations**: jm031/jm032 = 20 min (36000 frames), others = 30 min (54000 frames).\\n\\n## Decoder Performance\\n\\n- **Sample (2 sessions)**: Balanced accuracy = 0.41 (chance = 0.20)\\n- **Full dataset**: See train_decoder_full_out.txt\\n\\n## Decisions and Justifications\\n\\n1. **No additional cell filtering**: Track2p data already contains only matched, verified cells.\\n2. **Global percentile bins**: Motion energy discretized using global quintiles to ensure balanced classes across the full dataset.\\n3. **Maximin baseline**: Used Suite2p's default 'maximin' method for dF/F baseline, as the paper states \\\"default Suite2p parameters.\\\"\\n4. **Linear interpolation for missing frames**: Simple and appropriate for small gaps (1-3 frames typical). Larger gaps (116-148 frames in 2 sessions) also interpolated, which may introduce minor artifacts but preserves temporal alignment.\\n5. **2-minute trial blocks**: Matches the paper's cross-validation blocking strategy for decoding.\\n\"\n  5284→        }\n  5285→      }\n  5286→    },\n  5287→    {\n  5288→      \"step_id\": 72,\n  5289→      \"timestamp\": \"2026-07-29T02:48:59.510Z\",\n  5290→      \"source\": \"agent\",\n  5291→      \"model_name\": \"claude-opus-4-6\",\n  5292→      \"message\": \"Executed Write toolu_011vPraDLETt9DHC2apaDtgZ\",\n  5293→      \"tool_calls\": [\n  5294→        {\n  5295→          \"tool_call_id\": \"toolu_011vPraDLETt9DHC2apaDtgZ\",\n  5296→          \"function_name\": \"Write\",\n  5297→          \"arguments\": {\n  5298→            \"file_path\": \"/app/README.md\",\n  5299→            \"content\": \"# Neural Decoder: Track2p Barrel Cortex Motion Energy\\n\\n## Overview\\n\\nThis project converts longitudinal calcium imaging data from Majnik et al. 2025 (Track2p) into a standardized format for neural decoding. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Processing involves three steps: (1) Neuropil correction: Fc = F - 0.7*Fneu; (2) Baseline estimation using Suite2p's \"maximin\" method: Gaussian smooth (sigma=window/6), then minimum filter, then maximum filter over a 60-second window (1800 frames); (3) dF/F = (Fc - F0) / F0. After dF/F computation, the data is temporally binned by averaging 10 consecutive frames (30Hz to 3Hz). Final data is stored as float32.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_dff(F, Fneu):\n    Fc = F - NEUCOEFF * Fneu  # NEUCOEFF = 0.7\n    win = int(WIN_BASELINE_SEC * FS)  # 60s * 30Hz = 1800 frames\n    Flow = gaussian_filter1d(Fc.astype(np.float64), sigma=win / 6, axis=1)\n    Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\n    F0 = maximum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\n    F0_safe = np.where(np.abs(F0) < 1e-6, 1e-6, F0)\n    dff = (Fc - F0) / F0_safe\n    return dff\n\ndef bin_array(data, bin_size):\n    n_bins = data.shape[1] // bin_size\n    return data[:, :n_bins * bin_size].reshape(data.shape[0], n_bins, bin_size).mean(axis=2)\n\ndff = compute_dff(F, Fneu)\ndff_binned = bin_array(dff, BIN_SIZE)\n```\n\niii. The agent initially tried an 8th-percentile baseline filter but it was too slow (killed after 30+ minutes). The agent then switched to Suite2p's default \"maximin\" baseline method, reasoning that Suite2p's actual default method uses minimum_filter1d and maximum_filter1d which are much faster. The agent cited Suite2p source code for this method. The neuropil coefficient of 0.7 and 60-second window are Suite2p defaults as stated in the paper.\n\n## 2-c. After alignment, both neural and motion energy data have the same number of frames and are binned identically.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, interframe_int, n_neural_frames):\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    median_ifi = np.median(interframe_int)\n    aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\n    neural_idx = 0\n    for i in range(len(me)):\n        if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\n            aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\n        neural_idx += 1\n        if i < len(interframe_int):\n            n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\n            neural_idx += n_dropped\n    nans = np.isnan(aligned)\n    if np.any(nans) and not np.all(nans):\n        x = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\n        aligned[nans] = np.interp(x[nans], x[~nans], aligned[~nans])\n    return aligned\n```\n\niii. No raw timestamps or timing files are used.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t_i in range(n_trials):\n    start_sec = t_i * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n    time_bins = np.linspace(\n        start_sec + BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,\n        start_sec + TRIAL_DURATION_SEC - BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,\n        TRIAL_BINS\n    )\n    input_trials.append(time_bins.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.float32))\n```\n\niii. Since the imaging frame rate is constant (30 Hz) and bins are regular (10 frames each), the time can be computed deterministically without needing raw timestamps.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time from start of experiment*?\n\ni. Time is computed as the center of each temporal bin, accumulated from session start. For trial `t_i`, the start time is `t_i * 120` seconds. Within each trial, `np.linspace` generates 360 evenly-spaced time values from the center of the first bin to the center of the last bin. The first bin center of trial 0 is at ~0.167s; the last bin center of the last trial is at ~1199.83s (20-min sessions) or ~1799.83s (30-min sessions).\n\nii.\n```python\nstart_sec = t_i * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC  # t_i * 120\ntime_bins = np.linspace(\n    start_sec + BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,  # center of first bin (~0.167s for trial 0)\n    start_sec + TRIAL_DURATION_SEC - BIN_DURATION_MS / 2000,\n    TRIAL_BINS  # 360\n)\ninput_trials.append(time_bins.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.float32))\n```\n\niii. The agent interpreted \"Time elapsed from the beginning of the experiment\" as time from the start of each recording session. Time values are continuous across trials within a session (not reset per trial).\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time from start of experiment* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time input is inherently aligned with neural data because it is synthetically constructed to match the exact bin structure. Each time value corresponds one-to-one with a neural data time bin. Both have the same number of timepoints (360 per trial).\n\nii.\n```python\n# Same TRIAL_BINS (360) used for both:\nneural_trials = split_into_trials(dff_binned, TRIAL_BINS)\n# Time input also has TRIAL_BINS points per trial\ntime_bins = np.linspace(..., TRIAL_BINS)\n```\n\niii. Since time is computed rather than measured, alignment is guaranteed by construction.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Motion energy is derived from `motion_energy_glob.npy` (global motion energy from videography) in the `move_deve/` subdirectory of each session. Additionally, `interframe_int.npy` (inter-frame intervals) is used for alignment to handle dropped video frames.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_raw = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\nifi = np.load(os.path.join(move_dir, 'interframe_int.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The agent identified these files from the `move_deve/` directory structure and the data README, which describes \"motion energy extracted from videography of spontaneous behaviour.\"\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. Processing involves: (1) Alignment to neural frames by detecting dropped video frames via `interframe_int.npy` (gaps > 1.5x median interval) and inserting NaN at gap locations; (2) Linear interpolation of NaN values; (3) Temporal binning by averaging 10 consecutive frames (matching neural data binning); (4) Discretization into 5 equal-percentile bins using global quintiles across all sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_aligned = align_motion_energy(me_raw, ifi, n_frames)\nme_binned = bin_array(me_aligned, BIN_SIZE)\n# ... later, after collecting all ME values:\nall_me_concat = np.concatenate(all_me_values)\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)\nbin_edges = np.percentile(all_me_concat, percentiles)\nbinned = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1])\n```\n\niii. The agent followed the paper's binning approach (\"averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\") and the task specification for discretization (\"normalized and discretized into five equal-percentile bins\"). The agent did not apply explicit normalization (e.g., z-scoring or min-max) before discretization, treating equal-percentile binning as the normalization step.\n\n## 4-c. How is `output` *Motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Motion energy is discretized into 5 categories (0-4, labeled Q1-Q5) using global quintile bin edges. Bin edges are computed from all binned motion energy values across all sessions and mice. `np.digitize` with the interior bin edges maps each value to a category (0 through 4). Each bin contains exactly 20% of the global data.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_me_concat = np.concatenate(all_me_values)\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, N_OUTPUT_BINS + 1)  # [0, 20, 40, 60, 80, 100]\nbin_edges = np.percentile(all_me_concat, percentiles)\n# Discretize:\nbinned = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1])  # 0 to N_OUTPUT_BINS-1\nsession_output.append(binned.reshape(1, -1).astype(np.int64))\n```\n\niii. The agent chose global (across all mice and sessions) quintile binning to ensure balanced classes overall. The verification output confirms exact 20% per bin globally. However, per-session distributions are very uneven (some sessions of jm046 have no data in lower bins).\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Motion energy is aligned to neural frames before binning using the `align_motion_energy` function. This function detects dropped video frames from `interframe_int.npy` (intervals > 1.5x median = dropped frame), inserts NaN at those locations to align with neural frame indices, then linearly interpolates the NaN values. After alignment, both neural and motion energy data have the same number of frames and are binned identically.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef align_motion_energy(me, interframe_int, n_neural_frames):\n    if len(me) == n_neural_frames:\n        return me.astype(np.float64)\n    median_ifi = np.median(interframe_int)\n    aligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\n    neural_idx = 0\n    for i in range(len(me)):\n        if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\n            aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\n        neural_idx += 1\n        if i < len(interframe_int):\n            n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\n            neural_idx += n_dropped\n    nans = np.isnan(aligned)\n    if np.any(nans) and not np.all(nans):\n        x = np.arange(n_neural_frames)\n        aligned[nans] = np.interp(x[nans], x[~nans], aligned[~nans])\n    return aligned\n```\n\niii. The agent noted that 9/41 sessions had missing video frames (1-148 frames missing). The data README states: \"In some recordings there might be some missing frames from the camera... can be interpolated over.\" The agent chose linear interpolation for missing frames.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Missing video frames (detected via `interframe_int.npy`) are handled by inserting NaN values at the positions of dropped frames and then linearly interpolating. For neural data, a division-by-zero safeguard is applied during dF/F computation (baseline values < 1e-6 are clamped). The agent verified that no NaN or Inf values remain in the final output.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Missing video frames:\naligned = np.full(n_neural_frames, np.nan)\n# ... insert known values, then interpolate:\naligned[nans] = np.interp(x[nans], x[~nans], aligned[~nans])\n\n# Division by zero in dF/F:\nF0_safe = np.where(np.abs(F0) < 1e-6, 1e-6, F0)\ndff = (Fc - F0) / F0_safe\n```\n\niii. The CONVERSION_NOTES document: \"Linear interpolation for missing frames: Simple and appropriate for small gaps (1-3 frames typical). Larger gaps (116-148 frames in 2 sessions) also interpolated.\" The agent also verified post-processing: \"Trials with NaN: 0, with Inf: 0.\"\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is the dF/F computation, which applies Gaussian smoothing, minimum filtering, and maximum filtering over a 1800-frame window for every neuron. The agent's initial approach (8th-percentile filter) was so slow it was killed after 30+ minutes. The replacement maximin method is faster but still involves three sequential 1D filter operations over the full time axis for each neuron. Loading the .npy files (especially F.npy which can be hundreds of neurons x 54000 frames) is also significant I/O.\n\nii.\n```python\nFlow = gaussian_filter1d(Fc.astype(np.float64), sigma=win / 6, axis=1)  # win=1800\nFlow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\nF0 = maximum_filter1d(Flow, size=win, axis=1)\n```\n\niii. The agent explicitly documented that the percentile_filter was \"very slow\" and switched to the maximin method for performance.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The `align_motion_energy` function uses a Python for-loop iterating over each motion energy frame to detect and handle dropped frames. This could potentially be vectorized using cumulative sum operations on the interframe intervals. The trial-splitting loop and per-trial time computation loop are also Python-level loops, though these are minor given the small number of trials.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Python loop in align_motion_energy:\nfor i in range(len(me)):\n    if neural_idx < n_neural_frames:\n        aligned[neural_idx] = me[i]\n    neural_idx += 1\n    if i < len(interframe_int):\n        n_dropped = int(np.round(interframe_int[i] / median_ifi - 1))\n        neural_idx += n_dropped\n\n# Python loop for time input:\nfor t_i in range(n_trials):\n    start_sec = t_i * TRIAL_DURATION_SEC\n    time_bins = np.linspace(...)\n```\n\niii. The agent did not discuss vectorization opportunities in the CONVERSION_NOTES.\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. The code performs two passes over motion energy data: (1) collecting all binned ME values during the main session loop, and (2) re-iterating over stored ME trial data to apply discretization after computing global bin edges. This two-pass approach is necessary for global percentile computation but involves storing and re-iterating all ME trial data. The `get_sessions()` function is also called twice for each mouse - once during the main loop and once when building session_info metadata.\n\nii.\n```python\n# First pass: collect raw ME trials\noutput_all_raw.append(me_trials)\nfor mt in me_trials:\n    all_me_values.append(mt)\n\n# Second pass: discretize\nfor session_me_trials in output_all_raw:\n    for me_trial in session_me_trials:\n        binned = np.digitize(me_trial, bin_edges[1:-1])\n\n# get_sessions called again in metadata:\n'sessions': get_sessions(os.path.join(data_dir, mouse)),\n```\n\niii.
